Arsenal have suffered a significant setback in their pursuit of RB Leipzig winger Antonio Nusa, with Fabrizio Romano suggesting a January transfer is unlikely unless the Gunners make an extraordinary offer.

The Norwegian international has emerged as one of Europe’s most exciting attacking prospects, attracting considerable attention following his impressive performances in Germany. Mikel Arteta is keen to strengthen his attacking options, and Nusa has reportedly become a priority target ahead of the winter transfer window.

Arsenal have been particularly short of quality options on the left flank following the departures of Gabriel Martinelli and Leandro Trossard. Although Christos Tzolis arrived during the summer, the Gunners could benefit from another versatile attacker capable of making an immediate impact.

Nusa has enjoyed an impressive campaign with Leipzig, registering five goals and seven assists across competitions. His pace, technical ability and creativity could make him an excellent addition to Arteta’s attacking setup.

However, securing his signature could prove considerably more complicated than Arsenal would have hoped.

On his YouTube channel, Romano said Leipzig have set a starting valuation of approximately €70-75 million for the 21-year-old.

The Bundesliga outfit considers Nusa one of the most exciting wingers available and has little intention of negotiating a reduced transfer fee.

More importantly, Leipzig would prefer to retain the Norwegian until the summer before considering suitable offers.

Romano believes a January departure remains unlikely, explaining that only an extraordinary financial proposal could persuade the German club to reconsider their position.

“My understanding is that RB Leipzig start at around €70-75 million for Antonio Nusa, so he’s quite expensive.

“Leipzig believe he’s one of the best wingers on the market, and they will not offer any discount for Nusa.

“It’s also important to say that Leipzig’s expectation is to continue with the player until the summer transfer window and then consider a potential exit if a good proposal arrives.

“They are not pushing to sell Nusa in January. I think the possibility of him leaving in January is not particularly concrete. It’s not easy. Either there is a completely crazy bid or, for Nusa, it’s going to be the summer.”

The Gunners have the financial resources to pursue an ambitious deal, but paying a substantial premium midway through the season might not be straightforward.

Nusa could add another dimension in the final third, particularly with his ability to beat defenders and create chances.

Nevertheless, Arteta’s side may have to remain patient and revisit their interest next summer if Leipzig refuse to change their stance.

HOA Trade Group Presses for More Protections After DC Shooter Targeted Building Manager

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The trade group representing homeowners associations is calling for stronger protections after a shooting in Washington, DC, left a community manager dead.

The Community Association Institute said that it would renew efforts support laws that address harassment, threats, and intimidation against community managers before they escalate into violence.

The group had pressed for such legislation in Nevada in 2025 and plans to do so at the federal level in 2027. New Jersey and Minnesota are also reportedly considering some legislation to address harassment.

Thursday's shooting in DC's Logan Circle neighborhood claimed the life of James “Jimmy” Stuart, manager of the Metropole and injured five others. The suspected shooter, Dr. Matt Wade Lambert, a resident of the complex, reportedly was in a dispute with the community over renovations he said damaged his penthouse unit.

“We mourn Jimmy's loss and extend our deepest condolences to his family, friends, colleagues, and the entire Metropole community,” the CAI statement reads. “Our thoughts are also with the other victims, residents, and building staff whose lives have been forever changed by this tragedy.”

Lambert was in the middle of a protracted legal dispute with his building's HOA at the time that the shooting happened.

Social median posts reputedly made by him criticized the community for months after he alleged repairs caused water damage and mold. Police say that, after Lambert killed Stuart and injured four others, he subsequently took his own life during a police standoff.

According to legal filings, Lambert had incurred damages topping $100,000 as a result of the HOA's "failure to take corrective action to remedy the moisture accumulation."

“No one should have to fear for their safety while doing their job or serving their community. Community association managers, building staff, and volunteer board members dedicate themselves to maintaining the places millions of Americans call home. They deserve to carry out those responsibilities without threats, harassment, intimidation, or violence,” the statement concludes.

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

COP31 Leaders’ Summit Spotlights Electrification & Transition Away from Fossil Fuels

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Antalya, Türkiye — The COP31 Leaders’ Summit concluded today, with several new announcements and speeches from heads of state and the UN Secretary-General. Developments included country endorsements of the COP Presidency’s 35×35 electrification pledge, growing support for the transition away from fossil fuels, and new pledges from philanthropies to the Tropical Forest Forever Facility, among others.

Following is a statement from Ani Dasgupta, President and CEO, World Resources Institute:

“One message came across loud and clear from world leaders: climate action has shifted from an environmental movement to a core economic and security imperative. The conversation is moving beyond high-level commitments to the practical challenges of how to transition away from fossil fuels while supporting workers and communities and how to put entire economies on a cleaner, more resilient path.

“Electrification and the transition away from fossil fuels rightly took center stage. These are two vital, intertwined goals, and progress on one must reinforce the other. Early support for the 35×35 electrification pledge is encouraging, but many more countries need to endorse it and turn their commitments into credible national plans backed by concrete policies and investment.

“President Lula deserves credit for keeping the transition away from fossil fuels at the top of the international agenda and delivering a global roadmap that countries can adapt to their own circumstances. Australia and Türkiye now have an opportunity to lead by example, putting forward their own transition plans and encouraging others to follow.

“Vulnerable countries delivered an equally clear message: access to affordable finance is critical to them implementing their climate ambition. High borrowing costs and unsustainable debt are holding back investment in the very countries that need it most. With the $1.3 trillion finance goal established, the focus is shifting to how all sources of finance can work together to deliver investment at scale and how we ensure affordable capital reaches those who need it most. There are encouraging signs, including an influx of pledges from philanthropies for the Brazil-initiated Tropical Forests Forever Facility.

“Adaptation and early warning systems received much-needed attention after a year of crushing heatwaves, fires, and floods. But that attention now needs to turn to the investment in systemic resilience as the best way to make economies and societies stronger and improve people’s lives. Building more resilient cities was a major focus and will take deep collaboration between national governments, cities, and states. Cities are on the front lines of delivering the climate solutions and essential services that make people’s lives better, and demonstrating these benefits is critical to accelerating the transition. Local leaders are on the front lines of climate impacts and the delivery of essential services that make communities safer, healthier, and more resilient, yet too often lack the resources to respond. Cities and local communities especially need greater access to adaptation finance.

“It was disappointing to see no dedicated ocean event at the Leaders’ Summit, despite over 90% of countries including ocean action in national climate plans. The ocean is on the front lines of the climate crisis, but it could also be one of our greatest allies in tackling it. Ocean-based solutions can provide up to one-third of the emissions cuts needed by 2050 to keep 1.5°C within reach. This ambition for ocean action must be reflected in any COP31 cover decision and translate into concrete progress, especially for vulnerable coastal and island nations.

“Some leaders also delivered a resounding defense of multilateralism, even amid deep geopolitical tensions. International cooperation on climate may be changing shape, but it is still alive and needed now more than ever. Even in a fractured world, leaders recognize that no country can secure its economic future, build resilience, or prosper without working together on shared climate and environmental challenges. That spirit of cooperation must now carry through the negotiations and deliver a strong, ambitious outcome at COP31.”

WRI is a global research organization that spans more than 50 countries, with offices in Brazil, China, Europe, India, Indonesia, and the United States. Our more than 450 experts and staff work closely with leaders to turn big ideas into action to sustain our natural resources—the foundation of economic opportunity and human well-being. Find out more at www.wri.org

Grazing sheep in fields with solar arrays

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Two new research papers shed light on the growing practice of grazing sheep in fields with solar arrays. One demonstrates that, in the right numbers, sheep grazing increases populations of beneficial insects like bees and lady beetles compared to mowing around solar panels. The other research describes a decision-making support tool to help farmers profitably grow their solar grazing herds.

A team from the College of Agriculture and Life Sciences (CALS) found that when two sheep per hectare graze around solar arrays, the abundance of wild bees, honey bees and lady beetles jumped by 38%, compared to a control plot that was mowed. The increase was driven primarily by an 83% increase in honey bees alone, according to the team’s findings in “Low solar-grazing densities support beneficial insects in agrivoltaic systems,” published Oct. 7 in Environmental Research Letters.

“Sheep grazing and beneficial insect habitat are two prominent conversation topics around solar development right now,” said Leana Zoller, the paper’s first author and a postdoctoral associate in natural resources in the Cornell CALS Ashley School of Global Development and the Environment. “But what’s been missing is research that treats them as parts of the same system. When we talk about sustainable solar development, integrating these discussions into a single framework is really helpful to farmers and community decision makers.”

For three years, Zoller and a team of entomologists, ecologists and animal and plant scientists tested various stocking rates – how many sheep per hectare are allowed to graze a certain plot of land – using Cornell’s teaching sheep grazing at the university-owned Cascadilla Community Solar Farm in Dryden, New York. At four sheep per hectare, they found beneficial insect abundance was similar to the mowed control site, while vegetation management effectiveness was greater than at two sheep per hectare. At eight sheep per hectare, vegetation management was maximized and sheep flock health remained high, but honey bee abundance dropped by 45%.

“We zeroed in on stocking rates because that’s a concrete number that sheep farmers understand and work with every day, and we want this research to be useful,” said Steve Grodsky, paper senior author and assistant professor of natural resources in the Ashley School. “With solar grazing, people, energy, agricultural animals, plants and insects are all part of this ecosystem. We’re trying to understand the connections between those pieces so that when solar developers, municipalities looking at solar development, or farmers are faced with a decision, they can have scientific information about potential trade-offs and synergies.”

Grodsky is also assistant unit leader of the U.S. Geological Survey New York Cooperative Fish and Wildlife Research Unit. Additional co-authors are sheep farmer and solar grazing consultant Nikola Kochendoerfer, Ph.D. ’20; Antonio DiTommaso, professor in the School of Integrative Plant Science; John Losey, professor of entomology; Scott McArt, Ph.D. ’12, associate professor of entomology; Paige Muñiz, formerly a technician in McArt’s lab; and Todd Ugine, Ph.D. ’04, research associate in Losey’s lab, all in CALS. Sarah Carson, director of Cornell’s Campus Sustainability Office, was key in coordinating site access between researchers and the solar company that manages the Cascadilla solar farm.

Another Cornell research team, from the Cornell SC Johnson College of Business, has developed a tool that helps farmers whose sheep graze in solar arrays to make better decisions about their operation. The team described the tool in “Flock Management for Solar Grazing,” which published Sept. 28 in Manufacturing & Service Operations Management.

After a chance encounter with Caleb Scott, Ithaca-based sheep farmer and founder of United Agrivoltaics, Vincent Slaugh, assistant professor at the Cornell Peter and Stephanie Nolan School of Hotel Administration and first author of the paper, began thinking through the economics and logistics of solar grazing. He recruited Linwei Xin, associate professor of operations research and information engineering, and Shane Henderson, Charles W. Lake, Jr. Professor in Productivity, both in the Cornell Duffield College of Engineering, to work on the topic.

Together, they developed a spreadsheet-based model that helps sheep farmers to optimize procurement and sales decisions, based on factors like target flock size, costs, anticipated revenues, and expected vegetation in particular solar sites; if a farmer doesn’t have enough sheep to keep the grass down, they’ll have to cut it some other way. It also helps solar developers understand growers’ needs and develop contracts that are more profitable and sustainable for all parties long-term. United Agrivoltaics has already incorporated the tool into its onboarding process.

Slaugh sees opportunity for U.S. shepherds to grow their operations – especially with solar contracts offering a steady source of income and animal feed. Current domestic sheep production for meat and wool doesn’t come close to meeting domestic demand; about half of all lamb and mutton consumed in the U.S. is imported, primarily from New Zealand and Australia.

“If we grow the sheep industry, will the American diet eventually include more lamb? Could that be a side effect of the solar energy revolution? I think the answer is very likely yes,” Slaugh said. “And then when that happens, we’d expect processors and supporting industries to come back, for banks to be willing to give more loans, and for supply chains to follow. All of that grows the economy, and rural economies in particular.”

Kochendoerfer, who grazes 700 ewes on solar installations in New York and Vermont, said that economy is already growing.

“I work with a lot of farms that are now scaling up because of solar,” Kochendoerfer said. “There is a new wave of sheep farmers who are capitalizing on this access to good land. With solar grazing, you get paid to graze as opposed to having to pay to graze. That leads to sustainable, meaningful economic income that these farmers can use to expand, build a barn, buy equipment or hire someone. It’s enabling a lot of folks to build sound, viable agricultural businesses.”

Funding for these research projects came from the Cornell Atkinson Center for Sustainability, the National Institute for Food and Agriculture and the National Science Foundation.

Krisy Gashler is a writer for the College of Agriculture and Life Sciences.

Robert Pattinson cast as next James Bond

📅 2026-11-16  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Robert Pattinson has been cast as the next James Bond in the upcoming film from director Denis Villeneuve. It will be the 26th James Bond film and the first since Amazon MGM took over the iconic franchise.

Pattinson will be the seventh actor to play the role. Monday's announcement — which came in part via an Instagram post — ends a long period of speculation about who would take over as "007" after Daniel Craig.

"James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honour of a lifetime," Pattinson said in a statement. "I couldn't be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team."

Pattinson has appeared in several big-ticket films this year, including Christopher Nolan's "The Odyssey" and psychological thriller "Primetime," and he'll soon appear in "Dune: Part Three," also directed by Villeneuve. Pattinson, who rose to fame with the Twilight movie series, is also in production for Matt Reeves' "The Batman Part II," a follow-up to the 2022 film based on the DC comic.

The upcoming Bond film will have a script by Steven Knight and will be produced by Amy Pascal, known for her work on the Spider-Man franchise, and David Heyman, who produced all eight Harry Potter films.

"We were surrounded by extraordinary talent, but there can only be one 007. I am overjoyed to welcome Robert Pattinson as our new James Bond. He has the timeless soul of 007 and brings a spirit that is completely new. I cannot wait to begin," Villeneuve said.

In 2022, Amazon bought MGM Studios, which had long produced the Bond movies, for roughly $8.45 billion. Amazon MGM then acquired creative control of the James Bond intellectual property rights in 2025, when it closed a deal with longtime producers Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson.

The film is still untitled, and Amazon MGM hasn't announced a projected release date.

The lost years of Lamar Jackson -- and the greatness still within reach

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

Some years, Lamar Jackson's thumb hits another player and is fine. Some years, his ankle just gets hit rather than rolled up on. And those years, he has been -- and will be -- a player who can win an MVP and perhaps a Super Bowl.

Jackson's latest injury was almost too cruel to believe. After missing the previous three and a half games with a high ankle sprain, the two-time MVP was finally back. So, too, was the Ravens' hope. Jackson had looked phenomenal -- MVP-level phenomenal -- in new offensive coordinator Declan Doyle's system. Not only was he back, but he was back in a big spot: against fellow MVP Josh Allen in a crucial matchup vs. Buffalo on the first day of November, with Baltimore trying to end a two-game losing streak.

And then, on the Ravens' first play, Jackson hit his thumb on Greg Rousseau's arm. Jackson looked down at his injured right thumb, trotted toward the sideline and then took a knee.

"Oh boy," NFL on CBS analyst Ross Tucker said.

Medical personnel surrounded Jackson, draped a towel over his hand and brought him down the tunnel, off the field.

What more was there to say? Lamar Jackson, one of the defining players not just of the era but of NFL history, was hurt. Again. And it was serious. Again.

Jackson officially landed on IR with a thumb injury that will require surgery. It is "potentially" season-ending, and with the Ravens 4-5 and losers of four straight, it's looking like a lost season in Baltimore. This is the fourth time in the last six years the 29-year-old has missed at least four games. It has robbed the Ravens of their Super Bowl aspirations. It has robbed the NFL of a superstar. And it has robbed Jackson, a Heisman Trophy winner and twice an NFL MVP, of even higher levels of all-time greatness.

On the positive side, the thumb injury is the first ailment to land Jackson on IR. He has never had an ACL tear, an Achilles tear, etc. On the negative side, Jackson has racked up so many injuries of so many different kinds that it seems like something is constantly getting in the way, whether it's nagging at him or keeping him out of games. He even said the ankle pain entering the Bills game last week was merely "bearable," not gone.

Since the start of last season alone, Jackson has had hamstring, ankle, toe, knee, back, and thumb ailments. Not all of those kept him out, but they add up. He admitted he came back from last year's hamstring injury too early, impacting his play all season.

And yes, for what it's worth, it's bad luck. If Jackson's hand hits Rousseau at any other angle, he might not hurt his thumb enough to need surgery. If a diving Jermaine Johnson II doesn't awkwardly trap Jackson's ankle to the turf in Week 3, Jackson might not miss the next three games. Josh Allen and Patrick Mahomes have both had hundreds of similar plays and, with the exception of Mahomes' torn ACL last year, avoided injury for the most part. Sometimes, it's simply human anatomy, something Jackson cannot change.

For as prolific a runner as Jackson is, he does an excellent job avoiding hits on those plays. None of his injuries have been on runs. It has just been a constant parade of misfortune. Unfortunately for Jackson and the Ravens, the NFL rolls on, whether you're lucky or not.

Perhaps time and injuries have taken their toll and blocked our collective memory from how incredible Jackson has been. That would be truly unfortunate.

In 2019, Jackson's second year of the league, he threw 36 touchdown passes and ran for 1,206 yards. In NFL history, there are ...

In fact, only one other player ever has posted 1,200 rushing yards and merely double-digit passing touchdowns in a season: Spec Sanders of the New York Yankees (football version) in the All-America Football Conference in 1947. But he only threw 14 touchdowns. Jackson did things modern football has never known.

Jackson became the youngest quarterback to win MVP and joined Tom Brady as the only unanimous MVPs in NFL history. There are many ways to measure individual season greatness, but Pro Football Reference's "Approximate Value" is as good as any. Jackson had an approximate value of 25 in 2019, tied for the second-best season on record; LaDanian Tomlinson's 2006 season, in which he scored an NFL-record 31 touchdowns, is the only one better.

Jackson was healthy in 2020 and had another strong season, including a second straight 1,000-yard rushing effort. He has as many 1,000-yard rushing seasons as all other quarterbacks in NFL history combined (Michael Vick and Justin Fields both have one).

Then the injuries started. In 2021, Jackson was on pace for another 1,000-yard rushing season, and the Ravens led the AFC North when Browns linebacker Jeremiah Owusu-Koramoah dove at Jackson's ankles after Jackson released a pass. Jackson got carted off and didn't play in any of the final four games. The Ravens lost all four and missed the playoffs.

The next year, Jackson sprained his PCL when his left knee got driven into the ground on a sack by the Broncos' Jonathan Cooper. He ended up missing six weeks -- the final five of the regular season and a wild card game, which Baltimore lost.

This was a strange one. John Harbaugh initially wondered if Jackson could be back the next week and added "If not, it'll be sometime after that shortly." Then, Ravens officials were confident Jackson would return for the playoffs, per ESPN, leaving Jackson to essentially rule himself out -- and give his own medical update -- via social media.

In 2023, though, Jackson was healthy and marvelous once again. Under new offensive coordinator Todd Monken, Jackson set career highs in completion percentage (67.2%), passing yards (3,678) and yards per attempt (8.0). He was still excellent on the ground with a team-high 821 yards. He won MVP and was one vote short of doing so unanimously.

He was somehow even better in 2024, with 4,172 passing yards and 8.8 yards per attempt. He threw 41 touchdowns and just four interceptions. He ran for 915 yards. He had already reached unprecedented levels. Now, he was reaching new ones, as the first player with:

His 10.25 touchdown-to-interception ratio was also the best ever in any 30-touchdown season.

Somehow Jackson didn't win MVP. He was a first-team All-Pro, but MVP went to Allen, an extreme rarity. Jackson would have been the youngest three-time MVP in NFL history. That's how good the start was.

From 2019 to 2024, Jackson ranked third in passer rating and touchdown-to-interception ratio and fourth in yards per attempt. He was also fourth in touchdown passes despite missing significant time in two seasons. He was also eighth in rushing yards and first in yards per carry.

He was the Shohei Ohtani of football. No one can be this good at two different aspects, right? Wrong. Jackson was truly exceptional, and he seemed to be getting better.

He had nine touchdowns and zero interceptions on an absurd 9.6 yards per attempt through three weeks of 2025. Then came the hamstring injury, which started an avalanche of injuries. The hits haven't stopped coming since.

If two things stand out as Jackson's career shortcomings, it's injuries and playoff performance. The two are somewhat intertwined. In 2021, Baltimore missed the playoffs. In 2022, Jackson missed the playoffs. Last year, his injuries derailed the Ravens, but they somehow got a Week 18 win-and-in game against the Steelers. Tyler Loop missed a game-winning field goal attempt. It's looking like he will not add to his postseason ledger this season, either.

Had Jackson been healthy in even two of those four lost seasons, is he a Super Bowl champ? Or does he at least have more postseason wins?

Jackson is one of nine quarterbacks to win multiple MVP awards since the 1970 AFL-NFL merger. Of those nine, Jackson has the second-fewest playoff wins (two) before his 30th birthday, only ahead of the famously late-blooming Steve Young.

Wins, it is said, are not a quarterback stat, and that can be true. Unfortunately for Jackson and the Ravens, both his play and his team's have declined in the postseason. The Ravens are 3-6 in the playoffs since drafting Jackson in 2018 (3-5 with Jackson as the starter).

Jackson lost in his playoff debut in 2018, but the real disappointment came after his 2019 NFL season. With the Ravens as the AFC No. 1 seed, he had 365 yards and a touchdown passing and 143 yards rushing against the Titans, but he also had two interceptions,  lost a fumble, and failed on two fourth-and-short runs.

The next year, Jackson earned his first playoff win, getting a measure of revenge on the Titans in the wild card round. But one week later, Baltimore went quietly in a 17-3 loss to Buffalo, most remembered for Jackson throwing a terrible end-zone interception that Taron Johnson returned 101 yards for a touchdown.

The INT that's tied for the longest pick-six in playoff history?This 101-yard return from Taron Johnson in the 2020 Divisional Round 🔥📺: #KCvsBUF — Sunday 6:30pm ET on CBS📱: Stream on #NFLPlus pic.twitter.com/Z9SsFryOEL

Jackson suffered a concussion on the final play of the third quarter and did not return.

His next playoff game came after his 2023 MVP campaign. He was excellent in a win over the Texans, but in the AFC Championship, he lost a fumble and had another awful end-zone interception, this time in the fourth quarter. The Chiefs won 17-10, and Jackson's two turnovers loomed large.

In 2024, the Ravens, supercharged by the Derrick Henry addition, were on the precipice of a return to the AFC Championship Game before falling to the Bills, 27-25. Jackson again had turnover issues -- a bad interception and an even worse fumble, the latter of which led the a Buffalo touchdown -- but that game is better remembered for a Mark Andrews fumble and, later, drop on the potential game-tying two-point attempt.

There have been tons of moments where supporting cast members have let Jackson down in crucial moments. Andrews' nightmare two seasons ago and Zay Flowers' fumble at the 1-yard line against the Chiefs certainly come to mind. There's a lack of game-changing plays from the defense: two turnovers forced in Jackson's seven postseason starts, and none in the past two playoff trips, when hopes were the highest. But it's undeniable that Jackson has been very poor in the playoffs, too.

The final stat -- turnovers -- is truly staggering ... and costly. Jackson's turnovers have cost the Ravens an estimated 6.86 points per game in the playoffs, basically a touchdown. The only quarterbacks with a higher rate out of the 26-player sample are Drake Maye, Justin Herbert, and Trevor Lawrence.

Unless there's a major turnaround, this stands to be the Jackson's fourth postseason-less season in the last six years. He can't fix a glaring hole in his resume if he can't even get there.

The quick answer is "yes." It might just look a little different.

The first order of business, assuming this season is over for all intents and purposes, will be Jackson's contract. Former NFL agent Joel Corry dove into that recently. Of note ...

Baltimore should absolutely want to keep Jackson, even with the injury history. Through three games this year, he led the league in yards per attempt and was on pace for his highest missed-tackle rate and highest yards per rush of his career, all while not running as much as earlier in his career.

They have also been truly unlucky with injuries. He was healthy in 2023 and 2024. Last year's hamstring and this year's ankle were unfortunate, but the thumb was a freak injury. Perhaps we should expect him to miss a few games every year from now on, but there's also a reasonable chance he doesn't.

Jackson will be 30 next year, but 30 is not ancient. John Elway won his first Super Bowl at 37. Matthew Stafford got his first at 34. Drew Brees was 31. Peyton Manning was 30.

Josh Allen is 30 and, like Jackson, hasn't been to a Super Bowl. Joe Burrow is about a month older than Jackson and hasn't won one.

A common budding misconception is that Jackson can't be a star as he gets older and he runs less and/or less effectively. But Jackson has proved to be a superb pocket quarterback. His development as a passer is as impressive as it gets. Since 2023, Jackson has the third-highest yards per attempt in the NFL on throws inside the pocket and released within three seconds -- aka without much improvisation going on.

Jackson may never reach the heights he was on the path to after being on the precipice of becoming the youngest three-time MVP ever in 2024. That's the sad truth about a player playing hurt and/or missing significant chunks of three years of his prime. Would he have another MVP? Two? A Super Bowl ring? We'll never know, and that's tough to swallow.

But there's a version of Jackson -- a little luckier, a little better in the playoffs, a lot better of a passer than he was his younger years -- that can still be a superstar for many years and get him back on a path that ends with him as an all-time great.

Saylor’s Strategy Keeps Buying Bitcoin, Adding 'A Little Orange' Through the Dip

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Saylor’s Strategy Keeps Buying Bitcoin, Adding 'A Little Orange' Through the Dip

Strategy kept buying Bitcoin through a brutal market stretch, adding 485 BTC worth roughly $33.4 million and repurchasing $228 million of its preferred stock, as chairman Michael Saylor urged the market to rally with a characteristically cheeky nudge.

The company disclosed the purchase in a filing Monday. The modest addition, small by the standards of a firm that once bought tens of thousands of coins at a time, lifted Strategy’s total holdings to 850,849 BTC as of Nov. 15, alongside $4.9 billion in U.S. dollar assets.

Strategy, the largest corporate holder of Bitcoin, remains far ahead of any other treasury company.

The buy caps a stretch of smaller, steadier accumulation paired with stock buybacks. Strategy recently added $82 million in Bitcoin while repurchasing $188 million of its STRC preferred shares, part of a shift toward what it calls a capital-management approach, buying back stock when it views the shares as undervalued rather than relentlessly stacking coins.

Bitcoin has slid sharply in recent days, trading well below the six-figure highs it touched earlier in the cycle, and the downturn has squeezed Bitcoin treasury firms whose valuations rise and fall with the asset.

Saylor, undeterred, posted the holdings update to X alongside the firm’s trademark Bitcoin tracker, and followed it with a quip aimed at the slumping market: “This dip could use a little orange,” a nod to Bitcoin’s signature color.

The purchase lands during a precarious moment for the treasury-company model Strategy pioneered. Index provider MSCI has finalized rules that could eject Bitcoin treasury firms like Strategy from its widely tracked equity indexes, classifying companies whose balance sheets are dominated by a single asset as “non-operating” and therefore ineligible.

Removal would force index-tracking funds to sell the affected shares, potentially pressuring prices and shrinking the premium these firms have long commanded over the value of their Bitcoin.

For now, Strategy is doing what it has always done, buying Bitcoin and talking it up, even as the market tests its conviction.

The combination of a small purchase and a sizable buyback underscores the balancing act: keep accumulating, defend the stock, and weather a downturn that has turned the once-unstoppable treasury trade into a far more fraught proposition.

How Are Hotels Paid When You Redeem Points, And For Elite Perks?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There’s a real thrill to redeeming points for stays at luxury properties, and getting outsized value. I’m sure many people have wondered about the economics of that. How much is the hotel getting reimbursed for your stay? Who is paying for any elite perks that you receive? Does the hotel care if you redeemed points or not? Let’s talk about those various points in this post…

The major global hotel groups (Hilton, Hyatt, IHG, Marriott, etc.) don’t own a vast majority of their properties. Instead, the companies have an asset light strategy, and have management or franchise contracts for the individual properties.

In some cases, large investment firms own hotels, and they may have properties belonging to all kinds of brands. In other cases, a hotel may even be family owned.

The way the major hotel groups make money is primarily through fees (they’re also increasingly directly monetizing their loyalty programs, but that’s a whole different topic). There’s typically an initial fee to open the hotel, and then on top of that, there are fees on ongoing revenue. The ongoing fees typically fall into one of two categories (and they’re typically a percentage of revenue):

Then any upside (or downside) typically falls with the hotel owner. So the major hotel groups are making money, regardless of whether a hotel is raking in the cash or hemorrhaging money.

Now, one of the reasons that consumers flock to certain hotel groups is because of their loyalty programs, which are massive marketing machines. A big part of the value proposition of these loyalty programs is the ability to redeem points.

As you’d expect, those points are redeemed centrally through the program, and then the loyalty program has to “buy” that room from the hotel. After all, the hotel owner wants to be compensated for each head in a bed, especially since they’re already paying a system fund fee, to fund these perks. That brings us to the question of how hotels are reimbursed…

As you’d expect, hotel groups don’t publicly disclose how they reimburse their properties when members redeem points. However, over the years we’ve been able to get some insights into this.

Sometimes guests are given folios that are actually intended for the loyalty program, while in other cases the reimbursement rates showed up online when they weren’t supposed to.

While the exact reimbursement policies differ by program, here’s the general concept:

So I’m totally making this up here, but if a hotel is charging $400 on a particular night, maybe the loyalty program would pay $80 for that room if occupancy is under 90%, while it might be paying $350 if occupancy is over 90%.

Note that the number of points you redeem for a stay doesn’t directly determine reimbursement for a hotel. In other words, a hotel doesn’t get paid X amount per redeemed point. Similarly, if a program offers a fifth night free on award redemptions, the hotel is getting reimbursed for all five nights, as it’s the loyalty program incurring the cost there.

Instead, points redemption rates are set by the loyalty program, and are intended to reflect the cost incurred by the program. That’s why in many cases you’ll find that redemption rates don’t correlate directly to how much a property would cost in cash, but rather also factor in average occupancy at a property.

A hotel loyalty program would probably rather you redeem your points at a $1,000 per night hotel that’s 50% full, rather than a $500 per night hotel that’s 95% full, because the latter redemption will cost the program more. Hotel loyalty programs use historical data to determine points pricing, and the costs they incur.

Let me actually give a real example from a recent stay. I recently redeemed points for a two night stay at a Wyndham Rewards property, at the cost of 30,000 points per night. The folio I was sent accidentally had the amount Wyndham had to pay, rather than the amount I had to pay.

Even though I was paying the same number of points both nights, the hotel was being reimbursed around $87 for one night, and $184 for the next night. Funny enough, while sitting in the lobby working the first morning, I overheard one front desk agent tell the other that they were fully sold out the following night, so that tracks. The first night was being reimbursed at a reduced rate, while the second night was being reimbursed based on something closer to the average daily rate.

If you have elite status with a hotel loyalty program, how does taking advantage of elite perks factor into this when redeeming points? Who pays for breakfast? Who pays for suite upgrades? Who pays for waived destination fees and parking?

The answer is that “it depends,” though here are a few general considerations:

As you can see, funding elite benefits is a combined effort between the loyalty program and hotels.

For those curious, generally individual hotels don’t know how you earned your elite status. So it doesn’t matter to them if you earned it through actual hotel stays, or just by holding onto a credit card. Presumably a front desk agent could look at your stay history, but they typically wouldn’t have any reason to do so, in this context.

This is a loaded question, and there’s obviously no single right or wrong answers. A few thoughts, though.

Hotel staff are trained by corporate to treat guests redeeming points the same way as those paying cash. Many hotel groups even train front desk staff to thank people for redeeming their points, since it reflects engagement in the program. Officially, you should expect exactly the same treatment as if you paid cash, and you absolutely shouldn’t feel sheepish about the fact that you didn’t pay cash for your stay.

Now, in practice that might not always be how things play out. There’s sometimes a back and forth between hotel owners and hotel groups. Hotel owners often want it both ways — they want the power of the major hotel group distribution channels and loyalty programs, without actually incurring the costs associated with it, whether it’s complimentary upgrades, points redemptions, or a decent breakfast.

Will you sometimes find a hotel that’s perhaps a bit condescending if you redeem points and have an issue? Absolutely, but it’s not how it should be, and it’s the exception rather than the norm. Some may remember my stay at the Aegon Mykonos, at the time a Marriott Autograph Collection property (the hotel has since… mysteriously disappeared?).

Long story short, I was downgraded despite using a confirmed upgrade, and the manager was completely unapologetic about it, and was unwilling to do anything meaningful. One of his defenses was that I was staying “complimentary,” and therefore shouldn’t be complaining.

The hotel industry is complex, given the various parties involved, from the hotel group to the individual hotel owners. Hopefully the above is a useful rundown of how reimbursement works when redeeming points. The important thing to understand is that hotels get paid a lot more when you’re redeeming points if the property is full.

When redeeming points, you definitely shouldn’t feel sheepish about it. You earned your stay, and the reason hotels belonging to major loyalty programs are able to command a revenue premium is because of the points and loyalty perks they offer.

What’s your take on how hotels are reimbursed for award stays? And do you feel like you’ve ever been treated differently when redeeming points?

'Tokenization Is Not the Finish Line': Jupiter to Let Users Borrow Against Bullish Shares

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

'Tokenization Is Not the Finish Line': Jupiter to Let Users Borrow Against Bullish Shares

Jupiter, the largest trading platform on Solana, is bringing Bullish's shares onchain as collateral, as part of a push to put tokenized assets to work.

The company will launch a dedicated, permissioned market on its lending platform, Jupiter Lend, starting with Bullish's NYSE-listed BLSH stock, Jupiter tweeted on Monday. More issuer tokens will follow, though the market won't be available in all jurisdictions, it added.

A NYSE-listed company is bringing its own shares directly to Jupiter Lend.

Today at Breakpoint, we announced our partnership with @Bullish to launch a dedicated, permissioned $BLSH market.

For the first time, Issuer Tokens can be used as collateral inside Solana’s… pic.twitter.com/rWWqwOcWmK

— Jupiter (@JupiterExchange) November 16, 2026

Unlike many tokenized stocks, which give holders a claim on a share, Bullish's issuer-sponsored token "is itself the share," Jupiter COO Kash Dhanda said in a presentation at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London.

Users will be able to borrow against them with fully onchain liquidations in an "institutional-grade lending market," and integrations with qualified custodians, he said. Bullish acquired Equiniti, one of the world's largest transfer agents, earlier this year, Dhanda noted.

Dhanda framed the announcements around what he called the "post-tokenization era." Most tokenized assets today are "parked, not productive," he said.

Of the roughly $460 billion in tokenized assets tracked by data platform RWA.xyz, only about $39.5 billion is actually onchain and can be moved off the issuer's platform, and just $3.4 billion is used in DeFi, according to DeFiLlama, Dhanda said, a "utilization gap" of 99%.

"There is this myth, and this is the thing that's misunderstood: 'If you put it on chain, they will come'," he said. "This is not the field of dreams," he added, arguing that, "Tokenization is not the finish line. It is actually the starting line."

Jupiter launched two other products alongside the Bullish market. The Jupiter Global Passport lets users verify their identity to buy permissioned assets, while keeping their information as private as possible using zero-knowledge data sharing, Dhanda said. It is live with KYC verification, with onchain credit scores and proof of wealth to follow, and the company is exploring licenses in different jurisdictions.

Jupiter Liquidity Services, meanwhile, will help asset issuers design their markets, seed initial capital, find market makers and integrate with Jupiter's routing so that their tokens are available across the platforms that use it. "You integrate once, you will be available everywhere," Dhanda said.

"Shallow credit markets don't drive liquidity on chain," he added. "If you cannot do more with your onchain asset than you can do with the off-chain version of it, you're probably not going to switch."

Jupiter says it has handled more than $500 billion in trading volume so far this year and paid out $162 million in yield. The Breakpoint announcements follow the company's launch last week of token baskets, yield vaults and a rebuilt portfolio, and come as other venues bring tokenized stocks to Solana. Trading platform Formation has notified the SEC of plans to trade tokenized U.S. stocks on the network.

Jupiter powers token swaps on The Information Exchange, Decrypt's trading platform.

Robert Pattinson's James Bond Casting Continues An Unfortunate Trend For Nicholas Hoult

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Robert Pattinson is officially the man of the moment. 2026 was already his year, of course, considering his acclaimed turns in everything from A24's "The Drama" to Christopher Nolan's "The Odyssey" to "Primetime" — with still another big appearance to come in December with "Dune: Part 3." That's on top of reprising his role as Bruce Wayne/Batman in Matt Reeves' "The Batman Part II" and, as of today, his official casting announcement as James Bond. But can we take a moment to pour one out for the equally-as-talented character actor doomed to be the runner-up far more often than he deserves? We're talking, of course, about Nicholas Hoult.

We've sort of been dreading this exact outcome the moment we first heard about the various finalists up for the world-famous MI6 agent. In all fairness, neither Nicholas Hoult nor his agent (nor his bank account) should shed any tears based on his career trajectory to this point. He's made an impression in a number of smaller roles over the years (check out "The Order" from 2024 for great example of that), all while working with some of the best directors around. He's even waded into the blockbuster arena a few times, first in the 20th Century Fox "X-Men" movies and most recently as Lex Luthor in James Gunn's DC Universe.

But, despite all that success, we have to point out how the "Mad Max: Fury Road" scene-stealer continues to get the short end of the stick. Adding insult to injury, this isn't even the first time he's watched Pattinson specifically land the coveted role he was eyeing. So here's to Nicholas Hoult, the perpetual underdog who'll always be a star in our hearts.

"Always the bridesmaid, never the bride" is probably too harsh to describe what's going on with Nicholas Hoult lately but, man, it reaches a point. Despite proving himself to have one heck of a screen presence throughout the last several years, whether it be as an agent of chaos in Yorgos Lanthimos' "The Favourite" in 2018 or as a leading man in 2019's "Tolkien" biopic or even the horror-heavy "Nosferatu," Hoult has clearly been aching for the unambiguous breakout role that would make him a household name among general audiences. With all due respect to his meme-factory performances in "The Menu" or "Superman," well, neither of those have quite cut it. (Although that one photoshoot for "The Great" alongside Elle Fanning still leaves rent-free in our minds. You know the one.)

Sadly, that trend continues with the James Bond announcement. Real ones know it all started when Hoult was in contention for the title role in Matt Reeves' "The Batman," reportedly the only other name considered along with Robert Pattinson. But wait, it gets worse. After losing out on that crown jewel for superheroes, Hoult managed to find himself in the running for Superman a few short years later for James Gunn's reboot — which he ended up falling just short of, as well, though he picked up the role of supervillain Lex Luthor in a fun bit of meta casting. Third time isn't the charm, however, as he's now once again looking up to Pattinson as our newest Bond. How much is one man supposed to endure???

We love you, Nicholas Hoult, even if Hollywood's casting directors apparently have it out for you.

Novo Nordisk's weight loss injection cut obesity in young children, trial shows

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

In the STEP Young trial, BMI fell an estimated 17.4% with semaglutide and rose 2.2% with placebo at week 68 among children 6 to under 12

Novo Nordisk shared results on Monday from a Phase 3 trial showing its once-weekly semaglutide injection, sold as Wegovy, lowered body mass index in children ages 6 to under 12 with obesity. The company presented the STEP Young data at ObesityWeek 2026 and published them in The New England Journal of Medicine.

At week 68, estimated BMI fell 17.4% from baseline among children taking semaglutide and rose 2.2% among those on placebo, assuming all children stayed on treatment, the company said. The estimated difference was 19.7 percentage points. Counting all children regardless of whether they stayed on treatment, BMI fell 13.5% with semaglutide and rose 2.7% with placebo, a difference of 16.2 percentage points.

If all children had stayed on treatment, 40.4% of those on semaglutide would no longer have been classified as having obesity, versus 0% on placebo. Regardless of adherence, the figures were 38.4% and 2.1%. Both groups were put on an eating plan with fewer calories and encouraged to move more for the length of the study.

The trial enrolled 167 children, and more than 85% had class II or III severe obesity at baseline, Novo said. Doses reached a maximum of 1.7 milligrams or 2.4 milligrams, depending on baseline weight. At week 104, BMI had fallen an estimated 9.4% with semaglutide and risen 8.4% with placebo when all children were counted. Novo said the week 104 results were not adjusted for multiplicity and cannot be used for inferences.

Safety and tolerability matched earlier Novo pediatric and adult semaglutide trials, and no unexpected safety concerns or concerns about growth or pubertal development emerged, the company said. The 2.4-milligram injection carries a boxed warning for possible thyroid tumors, including cancer.

"Effective treatments for severe childhood obesity may shift a child's trajectory back towards one of improving weight related health," Ania M. Jastreboff, a professor at Yale School of Medicine and director of the Yale Obesity Research Center, said in a statement.

Semaglutide injection is not approved in the U.S. for children under 12, and Novo said there is no guarantee it will become commercially available for that use. Wegovy is approved for obesity in children 12 and older, and no medications are approved for younger children, according to CNBC.

Close to half of the children in the trial had at least one obesity-related complication, among them hypertension, elevated cholesterol and sleep apnea, according to CNBC.

Dr. Andrea Traina, Novo's medical director lead of obesity, said the two-year study cannot show if starting therapy sooner alters how the disease unfolds over a lifetime, or what ongoing care would require once these children become adults, calling it "an evidence gap," according to CNBC.

Childhood obesity touched about 177 million young people between the ages of 5 and 19 around the globe in 2025, according to the World Obesity Federation, as cited in Novo's release.

United CEO Still Keen on Merger Prospects

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Months after American Airlines rejected his pitch for a merger, United CEO Scott Kirby remains convinced that U.S. carriers will have to consolidate to thrive.

“I think the biggest impediment is having a willing partner,” Kirby said at the Yahoo Finance Invest conference on Nov. 12. “I am more convinced today than I was when I wrote my note six months ago that it could get approval. It could get approval because it is good for customers.”

Kirby’s original pitch to American shocked the aviation industry, as it would have brought together two of the country’s largest commercial airlines and given the combined company a market share of just over 34%, a level of dominance not seen by any U.S. carrier in years. Analysts predicated that the Justice Department would reject the tie-up as anticompetitive, citing regulators’ decisions on much smaller attempted mergers over the past several years.

The speculation ended when American released a public statement saying it had no interest in a merger, and would stick to its existing turnaround plan.

But Kirby has continued to defend his proposal and the logic behind it, arguing that a consolidated United-American would deliver a better experience for customers, create more high-paying unionized jobs, and make the U.S. more competitive in international travel.

The CEO acknowledged Thursday that such a large deal would face obstacles but insisted they could be overcome.

“It wouldn’t be easy,” he said. “We’d have to do some divestitures, but I 100% think it could get approved.”

Airlines around the world are facing new pressure to cut costs or consolidate as jet fuel prices rise. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said in April that he believes there is “room for some mergers” in the airline industry, but did not mention United or American by name.

Neither the Justice Department nor the Transportation Department ever formally weighed in on the potential United-American merger, as a final deal was never filed. President Donald Trump said he would not support it, and cited competition and consumer prices as the reason.

My murderous wine merchant whines for a chance at republican rule in Crusader Kings 3’s Silk and Silver expansion, which isn't only about the money

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Longing for the Venetian Dogehouse, stuck in the Venetian doghouse

The tentacles of my trade empire snake up towards France in the west, down towards Africa in the south, and off into Hungary in the east. Yet, I still can’t reach out and grasp the throne that sits right in front of me. I’m Marco Sanudo, and the throne of the serene republic of Venice eludes me.

I’ve shed blood in its name, the blood of someone else named Marco no less. I’ve managed to avoid punishment for that crime, despite the fact it earned me a fiefdom to rule while I’ve tried to up my patrician score enough to be the lucky party the next time a ruling Doge dies in office (or runs out their 10 year term). Sadly, Doge Leonardo, the mysterious masked lord who’s had an iron grip on power since the first couple of old blokes to hold the position in this playthrough popped their clogs, is proving incredibly tough to oust.

It should, in theory, help that he’s my best mate, thanks to a well-timed feast. It certainly did in being granted a pardon for offing that other Marco, but not even that and a plague which infected nearly everyone in the realm aside from me have paved the way to Dogedom. In the meantime, I’ve striven to gain wealth and influence. I’ve worked up to having four trade routes running at once, with goods like wine, iron, and textiles flowing from market to market across southern Europe and bringing a steady supply of coin back to my Venetian estate. I’ve built trade posts at the extremities of my routes, giving me the option of starting from further afield and reaching new lands if the cash flow dries up closer to home.

This is where Crusader Kings 3’s Silk and Silver expansion has blended a bit with the game’s traditional feudal moneymaking to supercharge my coffers. The land holding I killed a dude for, the Proveditorate of Zara, means I’ve also had tax money from modern day Croatia to add to both my trade profits and the income my domicile generates on its own. I am, as the medieval kids say, minted.

Getting to the point of serious pocket clinkage is, as you might imagine given Silk and Silver’s focus on filling the game’s world with travelling merchant companies and wheeler-dealing republican patrician families, the beating heart of the expansion. A near-constant stream of notifications about prices of different goods I’m trying to hawk in different locations encourage me to regularly prune and re-work my routes so that max profits are being earned and no carrying capacity’s being wasted. The bubbling up of wars and plagues impacts prices in affected cities, making it a never-ending battle if you’re desperate to optimise everything within an inch of its life. I’ve found that degree of micromanagement unnecessary if the goal is just to keep a decent profit margin ticking over. Just remember to replace the trader assigned to a route if they die, recall them every few years so they don’t stress themselves to death, and check in every so often to re-evaluate whether you’re running goods that are suitably in demand.

If you stick at it for a decent amount of time, with a certain type of good - alcohol or jewellery, for example - your expertise in dealing in them increases, making it easier to sell for better prices. Trade routes left running between the same markets also gain boons from being in place for a long time, offering a counterpoint to rejigging them every five seconds to wring out a few more coins. Most of my routes have run along a path that includes stops at four markets, with the occasional location boasting a monopoly I’ve bargained for with the local ruler, so I don’t have to worry about competition at every stop.

When you’re playing as a merchant who isn’t locked into the ruling inner circle of a republic, making your routes as lengthy as possible is one of the three moneymaking areas your company can specialise in. That first one’s caravaneering, and is the one I leant into in a non-Marco Sanudo playthrough. Beyond that, you can become an expert loan shark, lending cash to rulers or paying their ransoms in order to recoup interest and collect hooks you can use for favours. Manufacturing expensive artifacts that can be sold individually to collectors for a profit is the third option. The latter two I’ve only dabbled in a little bit as of writing, having earned enough gold mostly from caravanneering to buy the land containing the city with my home market and be anointed a Sheikh. So, beginning as a landless merchant is a nice alternative to Crusader Kings 3’s long-established wandering adventurer starting point, with both allowing you to work your way up from lowly serf to landed lordly dynasty in your location of choice.

If you’re after a more specific historical scenario, Silk and Silver has Guelphs and Ghibellines. This is where we find Marco Sanudo, as well as the other patrician family head I’ve played as thus far, Speronella Dalesmanni. The setup is that the Pope and the Holy Roman Emperor have had a bit of a tiff, essentially rooted in who should have certain powers, with different cities and powerful families in northern Italy taking either the Guelph (Pope-likers) or Ghibelline (Holy Roman Empire-enjoyers) side depending on what suits their goals or principles. I’ll be honest, aside from being another way to easily earn friends or enemies in the ruthless crucible of merchant republican politics, where votes and reputation-bolstering aid from other nobles are key, the situation’s not had a hugely obvious bearing on my playthroughs thus far.

I think the closest it’s come is during my Dalesmanni run, if largely due to the existence of the By God Alone expansion allowing a new branch of Catholicism, dubbed Italian Christianity, to be created. The initial conversion push from the bishop responsible led a number of my fellow northern Italian nobles away from the Roman Rite headed by his Popeliness, making the Guelph/Ghibelline split more clearly defined.

In the early hours of both my Sanudo and Dalesmanni playthroughs, I’ve been far more focused on simply trying to push the respective patrician families up the hill from posh toffs with naught but a vote and a manor to elected rulers of their great cities, with land holdings to match. Speronella’s situation in Padua is made incredibly tough by the fact she’s a woman in a world where every succession rule is designed to put blokes first, so I’ve had more success with Marco and his quest to become the Doge of Venice.

As the head of a patrician family, one of select few elite clans who steer the oligarchical republic, I was faced with earning Marco enough respect among his fellow family heads that they’d consider voting him to the top of the succession list, thus securing his power whenever the current Doge got the boot. Pulling that off, while also not kowtowing to the requests and schemes of those same peers, isn’t easy. A large cocktail of factors go into determining whether another noble likes you enough to vote in support of your succession. There’s your patrician rating, which is upped by doing things which specifically show you to be good at this Italian noble house lark, such as establishing trade monopolies for the republic, gaining the Pope’s favour, or having close relatives who also hold key positions in the republic. However, how close a friendship you have with the target and how their character traits align with you also play a huge rule.

Thus it was that, at one point, Marco was best mates with the current Doge and had a decent patrician rating, but had only amassed 68 of the 72 points needed for said Doge to vote for him because the Doge was generous, and therefore didn’t care for Marco’s ambitiousness. That same Doge either liked or respected Marco enough to grant him a council position and the status of regent, but he would not back Marco to replace him permanently for love nor money. This is the same man who liked Marco so much that when I pulled off that not-so-sneaky murder on another noble house head in order to inherit the keys to the Proveditorate of Zara, the Doge was instantly willing to pardon me and prevent Marco suffering any punishment for the crime.

Not even a couple of serious plagues hitting Venice, which at one point had all three people ahead of Marco in the queue suffering from smallpox, could get him on that throne. Eventually, it took a bit of lucky timing when his Doge mate died of old age - a few years after beating smallpox - to finally install Marco as Doge. Given that delayed promotion, as of writing I’ve not yet had enough time in the big chair to give a proper verdict on how it differs from regular feudal rulerdom in the long run.

What I can do instead is tell you a bit about the fun of council votes. The same pool of voters who in a republic like Venice, get a say in who succeeds the ruler, also vote on the passage of laws and decrees. One proposal you can put forward is a vote to change who exactly is allowed a seat in this voting pool. It defaults to just being heads of patrician families, but can be extended to include all vassals, or tightened up so only the Doge’s councillors or the ruler themselves have a say, that being the way despotism lies. As you can imagine, passing a change to who can vote can drastically change the state of play, either tightening up the number of folks who might oppose or support you, or bringing new folks into play in a fashion that dilutes individual power, but gives you more pieces on the board to mind or manipulate to your advantage.

I’ve found it suitably tough to orchestrate successful votes to change laws and customs which medieval Italian noble families, expectedly, have firmly entrenched stances on. Getting rid of checks and balances, such as the requirement to vote on banishments or wars - so the Doge can rule as a despot - has proven one such challenge, as has changing laws governing men being prioritised over women. I tried to pass equal succession as a substitute for men being put first while playing as Speronella, but met an insurmountable wall of resistance due to the region’s culture and faith. So, all I could do was try to put my young male heir in the best position possible to make the sorts of gains I couldn’t.

Much easier to weasel into place, either through some canny swaying or the offering of hooks, are laws giving state backing or other priority to trading in certain types of goods. Alcohol, for instance, which certainly hasn’t hurt the profits of Marco’s two wine trade routes.

Thus, cash continues to flow through the tentacles of my trade empire in sizable globs. As he’s now Doge, I’ve got Marco using the ample funds of Venice’s treasury to build a new piazza and have him focused on making the city’s already-strong trade league even more formidable, by attracting new members from the few other northern Italian blocs.

While I’m fairly sure there are folks who’ll find Silk and Silver's constant barrage of trade route notifications annoying, or that their power-grabbing skills and sheer multitude of money-making means combine to make playthroughs less challenging than they could be, so far I've found it another enjoyably sonorous string to Crusader Kings 3’s bow. Above all else, playing as a merchant gives more reason to invest in stewardship skills beyond just maximising your tax yields, while the republican politics of the DLC's main region fleshes out rewarding intrigue-focused builds. Diplomacy and martial skills are less key, but still have their place at times.

If you’re an absolute stickler for historical accuracy, you may well find other things to gripe about, but aside from the Guelph and Ghibelline conflict fading into the background, I’ve found plenty to like in my quest to become top Doge.

How Much Does The World's Largest Commercial Engine Cost?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The headline figure that the incoming GE Aerospace 134-inch GE9X turbofan is worth $42-45 million is misleading. Understood as a list price, it is ok, but compared with the market value of a GE90 or Trent XWB, it is not. While the engine is likely to be the most expensive engine on the market, it is still likely to belong to the same tier as the competing Trent XWB-97, with the difference in value measured in single digits of millions of dollars, not tens of millions.

It is also incredibly difficult to disentangle the price of an engine from its financing, airframe, status as a bundled spare, long-term maintenance contracts, and bulk orders. It is almost unheard of for an airline to walk into a GE Aerospace showroom, point to a large aero engine, and pay upfront for one engine with no maintenance, tooling, spare components, or other services. Here is how much a GE9X is worth in 2026, attempting to avoid apples-to-oranges comparisons.

The GE Aerospace GE9X is the world's largest commercial jet engine by diameter, and by thrust produced during test runs, it is not the world's largest by certified thrust. The world's largest aeroengine by certified thrust is its predecessor, the GE90-115B. The GE90-115B was built to power the Boeing 777-200LR, Boeing 777-300ER, and Boeing 777F freighter. It comes with a certified thrust of 115,300 lbf at sea level.

Its GE90-94B counterpart is certified for 93,700 lbf and powered the previous Boeing 777-200 and Boeing 777-300 generation of Triple Sevens. GE Aerospace says it has test-run the new GE9X generation of the GE90 family at 134,300 lbf. It claims that this is the "highest thrust of any commercial jet engine." But producing the highest thrust in test runs and actually being certified for that are two different things.

The GE9X has a certified thrust of 110,000 lbf, placing it at 5,300 lbf less than its predecessor. GE Aerospace's new variant will provide the incoming Boeing 777X family with 5% fuel-specific fuel consumption improvement versus "any twin-aisle engine available" (read: Rolls-Royce Trent XWB), and offers up to 10% specific consumption improvement versus the older GE90-115B. It achieves an approximate 10:1 bypass ratio.

Safe Fly Aviation writes, "Aircraft engine values are among the most important metrics in commercial aviation finance. A single powerplant can cost more than a private jet, and in today's market, some engine types are worth more than the aircraft they power." According to its analysis, the General Electric GE9X is the world's most valuable engine at around $42–45 million at list price. It says, "Its list price of $42–45 million per engine exceeds the list price of many private jets, making it the pinnacle of aircraft engine values."

For reference, it lists the GE90-115BL as having a "Full-Life value" of over $32 million and a Half-Life Market Value increasing by 60% since 2023. The only other extra-large modern engine is the Rolls-Royce Trent XWB, "valued at approximately $25 million per unit." There are some glaring issues with these comparisons, and they do not offer an apples-to-apples comparison.

The GE9X is not yet on the market, as the Boeing 777X is yet to enter service. Therefore, the only data point is its list price, while the market values for the other two engines are being used. I have an accounting background, and this is a terrible comparison. List prices are little more than brochure listings where manufacturers and sellers often inflate the true market value of their products. The market value, what companies actually pay, is much more consequential.

Discover why the Boeing 777X depends entirely on the GE9X, the world’s largest and most advanced commercial jet engine ever built.

In its H2 2026 update, IBA states, "Current widebody engine values continue to grow strongly due to new aircraft production delays. Existing engine retention remains high, MRO demand is strong, and spare engine supply is limited." The value of an engine is not just its performance or the cost of manufacture, but also its scarcity. This is the classic economics 101 of supply and demand. Currently, demand is high, and supply is constrained, driving up costs.

Put another way, the delay of the Boeing 777X and the fact that the GE9X is not yet in service is making GE90-115BL and Trent XWB engines more valuable. Airlines are being forced to keep their older GE90-powered 777s in service for longer, which necessarily includes the engines. Low spare engine availability for the GE90-115BL is driving up the value of the engine.

GE Aerospace (revenue-sharing partners: IHI Corp., Safran, MTU Aero Engines)

From $18 million+ (line-fit for Emirates) to $37 million (standalone spare engine)

While the A350 is a new aircraft with no airline seeking to retire them, the fact that it is one of the only larger widebodies available, given production constraints of the 787 and no 777Xs, is driving up their value. IBA states that the GE90-115BL full-life values exceed $35 million, and its long-term lease extensions are being observed between $250,000 and $300,000 per month. Meanwhile, MBA Aviation lists the H1 2026 lease rates for the Trent XWB-97 with a market lease rate of $287,000.

In commercial aviation, virtually no airline or lessor pays the published Original Engine Manufacturer (OEM) list price for a factory-new widebody aero engine. In other words, the GE9X is not really worth $42 million. GE Aerospace can slap any value that it claims to value it at, but that is divorced from the reality of the market. The 'real' price is the 'discounted' price that airlines and lessors actually pay for them.

As a rule of thumb, when engines are purchased as part of a new aircraft order, especially on competitive twin-engine aircraft, OEM's routinely provide 'concessions' (aka discounts) of 40% to 46% or more off their inflated catalog list prices. However, the discount is typically less on aircraft like the A350 or 777, where Rolls-Royce or GE Aerospace are exclusive engine suppliers. That said, there are still typically substantial discounts.

The true value of the GE9X will become much more apparent when it is in service and starts appearing in secondary markets. Until then, there is simply a lack of data compared with the XWB and GE90. The value of the engine also varies by whether it comes as part of the aircraft or separately. This is similar to a car. It is typically much cheaper to purchase a car fully assembled and ready to go than purchasing each individual part, including the engine, and then self-assembling it.

The largest and most powerful commercial engine ever built, the GE9X is by no means cheap

Speaking of list prices per seat (not engines per se), Sirius Capital Aviation writes, "list prices were far higher than any realistic estimate of actual prices (typical discounts were already over 50%) but they did provide a limited market signal of relative prices." Put another way, the list price of $42 million for the GE9X is a starting figure that needs to be discounted to find out what it is really worth. It is absolutely not a number that can be compared in good faith with the market price of a Trent XWB-97.

Another issue is that it can't even be compared with XWB list prices, as Rolls-Royce does not publish standalone engine list prices and Airbus ceased publishing its annual aircraft list price catalog in 2018. Using a reverse calculation, the implied list value for the XWB would likely fit into the $35 to $40 million range. This is back-of-the-envelope math and should not be taken too seriously. Given that the GE9X is the sole engine for the 777X, an expected discount off list price of around 35% to 40% can be expected.

Importantly, the GE9X may be the only engine on the 777X, but the 777-9 still has to compete with the XWB-powered A350-1000. If this is the correct range, then the real implied market rate of around $22 million to $28 million per engine can be expected. It is possible that 35-40% is too steep, and so the market price may be higher. In other words, the value of new XWB and GE9X engines may be within a reasonably modest margin of each other.

Also, engines are seldom just purchased alone. They are part of bundles that include other engines, the aircraft themselves, spares, and service agreements. Engine manufacturers may sell engines at or even below manufacturing cost, as their margins are made up on the lucrative 15–25-year-long servicing of the engines. Spare engines purchased directly from the OEM generally get smaller discounts than line-fit installed engines.

Even looking at announced contracts is problematic. For example, GE announced Singapore Airlines had ordered 22 more GE9X engines in 2022 for $2.8 billion at list price. That works out to more than $100 million per engine, but it also includes a 12-year maintenance contract (called TrueChoice). The price of engines likely varies considerably by airline.

Emirates has placed orders for around 45% of the entire global 777X backlog. This shifts much of the bargaining power to Emirates from Boeing and GE. Much of the program is reliant on Emirates (and Qatar), and Emirates knows it. While the numbers are not available, it is reasonable to assume Emirates is purchasing GE9X engines for much less than Silk Way West, which is ordering two aircraft with four engines.
